I’ll tell myself it’s probably nothing, but then I spend the whole day worrying I’m letting myself get fat, and I start thinking about how I’m going to make up for it.\n\nI know that probably sounds a bit extreme. But yes, I think the fear of gaining weight is a lot of what’s behind it.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period where you didn't have these episodes of eating when you can't stop and then making yourself throw up?\n\nPatient: I guess… not really, at least not lately.\n\nFor the last few months — maybe four months or so — it’s been pretty consistent. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Um… thanks. I guess I’m here because my eating has been feeling kind of out of hand lately. Mostly when I’m alone at night—once I start, it’s like something takes over, and I can’t really stop, even though I’m telling myself to just put the food away. I know that probably sounds… I don’t know, but it’s been worrying me.\n\nDoctor: How do you feel after one of these episodes of eating when you can't stop?\n\nPatient: I feel… really awful, honestly. Embarrassed, mostly. And panicky, like I’ve messed everything up.\n\nUm… I don’t really like saying this, but after it happens I usually go to the bathroom and make myself throw up. It feels, in the moment, like the only way to sort of… undo it before the calories settle in. I know that’s not good. I’m sorry, I’m trying to be honest.\n\nDoctor: How often do these episodes of eating when you can't stop happen?\n\nPatient: Um… I guess it’s been more than just occasional. For the last four months or so, there’s usually been at least one night a week where I’m by myself and I eat way more than I meant to. And then… afterward I make myself throw up. I know that’s not great, but that’s the pattern, I think.\n\nDoctor: Do you worry about gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, probably more than I should. I try to tell myself it’s just normal concern, but if the scale is even like a pound up after a shift, or my work pants feel a little tighter, it can kind of ruin my whole day.\n\nI start thinking I’m letting myself get fat, and then I’m… planning how I’m going to make up for it. I know that probably sounds a bit much.\n\nDoctor: Is making yourself throw up mostly to prevent weight gain?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I don’t like admitting that, but after I eat like that, I usually end up in the bathroom making myself throw up because it feels like the only way to undo it before the calories… settle in, I guess.\n\nI know that probably sounds bad. I’m not trying to be dramatic — it just feels really urgent in the moment.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight or body shape is different from how others see it?\n\nPatient: I… yeah, I think so. I mean, I know other people probably don’t look at me and see what I see, or they’d say I’m being too hard on myself.\n\nBut when I’m in the bathroom mirror at home, it’s like all I can focus on is my stomach and my waist. If they look… wrong, or bigger than I think they should, I feel like I’ve messed up somehow, like I wasn’t disciplined enough. Even if I had a good day at the rescue center and did everything right there, that part can still make me feel like I failed.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over the past few months?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure, honestly. I mean, I check, but it kind of goes up and down, and I don’t know if I’d call it “significant.” Maybe it feels significant to me when it changes a little, but I’m not sure it would look that way to someone else.\n\nIs that… the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever exercise excessively to try to burn off calories after one of these episodes?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in a big way. I mean, I might walk around more or feel restless afterward, but I wouldn’t say I’m doing intense workouts to make up for it.\n\nAt least, I don’t think that’s what I’m doing. Is that what you mean by excessively?\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of gaining weight the main reason you try to undo the calories after eating?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that’s probably the biggest part of it, even if I don’t like admitting that.\n\nLike, if I get on the scale and it’s even a pound higher after a shift, or if my work pants feel tighter, I kind of latch onto it. I try to tell myself it’s just normal concern, but if the scale is even like a pound up after a shift, or my work pants feel a little tighter, it can kind of ruin my whole day.\n\nI start thinking I’m letting myself get fat, and then I’m… planning how I’m going to make up for it. I know that probably sounds a bit much.\n\nDoctor: Is making yourself throw up mostly to prevent weight gain?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I don’t like admitting that, but after I eat like that, I usually end up in the bathroom making myself throw up because it feels like the only way to undo it before the calories… settle in, I guess.\n\nI know that probably sounds bad. I’m not trying to be dramatic — it just feels really urgent in the moment.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight or body shape is different from how others see it?\n\nPatient: I… yeah, I think so. I mean, I know other people probably don’t look at me and see what I see, or they’d say I’m being too hard on myself.\n\nBut when I’m in the bathroom mirror at home, it’s like all I can focus on is my stomach and my waist. If they look… wrong, or bigger than I think they should, I feel like I’ve messed up somehow, like I wasn’t disciplined enough. Even if I had a good day at the rescue center and did everything right there, that part can still make me feel like I failed.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over the past few months?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure, honestly. I mean, I check, but it kind of goes up and down, and I don’t know if I’d call it “significant.” Maybe it feels significant to me when it changes a little, but I’m not sure it would look that way to someone else.\n\nIs that… the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever exercise excessively to try to burn off calories after one of these episodes?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in a big way. I mean, I might walk around more or feel restless afterward, but I wouldn’t say I’m doing intense workouts to make up for it.\n\nAt least, I don’t think that’s what I’m doing. Is that what you mean by excessively?\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of gaining weight the main reason you try to undo the calories after eating?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that’s probably the biggest part of it, even if I don’t like admitting that.\n\nLike, if I get on the scale and it’s even a pound higher after a shift, or if my work pants feel tighter, I kind of latch onto it. I’ll tell myself it’s probably nothing, but then I spend the whole day worrying I’m letting myself get fat, and I start thinking about how I’m going to make up for it.\n\nI know that probably sounds a bit extreme. But yes, I think the fear of gaining weight is a lot of what’s behind it.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period where you didn't have these episodes of eating when you can't stop and then making yourself throw up?\n\nPatient: I guess… not really, at least not lately.\n\nFor the last few months — maybe four months or so — it’s been pretty consistent. Usually at least one night a week, when I’m by myself, I end up eating way more than I meant to, and then afterward I make myself throw up.\n\nI don’t love saying that. I keep wanting to say it’s not that bad because it’s not every day, but… yeah, it has been happening regularly.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel a lack of control during these episodes of eating when you can't stop?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I wish I could say it’s just poor planning or being hungry, but once I’m alone at night and I start eating, it kind of feels like something takes over.\n\nI’ll be telling myself, “Just put it away, stop now,” and I still keep going. It’s embarrassing to admit, but yes, it feels like I’m not really in control in those moments. Is that… is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you also restrict your food intake between these episodes of eating when you can't stop?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that.\n\nI don’t think I’m, like… formally restricting, if that makes sense? I don’t have a strict plan or anything. Sometimes I do try to “be good” the next day or eat lighter, but I haven’t really paid close attention to whether it’s a pattern.\n\nIs that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you often eat very little during the day before one of these episodes of eating when you can't stop happens?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I haven’t really tracked it that closely.\n\nI mean… some days I might be busy at work and not eat much, but I don’t know if that’s specifically before it happens or just normal being busy. I wouldn’t want to say yes if I’m not sure.\n\nI can try to pay more attention to that, if that would help.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods of significant weight loss followed by weight gain over the past year?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in any big, obvious way.\n\nMy weight maybe goes up and down a little, but I haven’t noticed, like, a major drop and then gaining it all back or anything. I don’t weigh myself super consistently, so I might not be the best judge, but nothing that stood out to me as significant.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a time when you lost a lot of weight and then gained it back?\n\nPatient: Not that I can really think of, no. I mean, my weight has probably shifted a bit here and there, but I don’t remember a time where I lost a lot and then put it all back on.\n\nI hope that answers it—I’m not trying to be vague, I just don’t think there’s been anything that dramatic.\n\nDoctor: Do you often skip meals or go for long periods without eating on purpose?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I do it on purpose, no. I mean, I might miss a meal if work gets hectic or I’m distracted, but I wouldn’t say I’m intentionally trying to go a long time without eating.\n\nAt least… not that I’ve really noticed. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you need to exercise a lot to burn off the calories after one of these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I’m not someone who goes and works out for hours after eating or anything like that.\n\nSometimes I might feel like I should move around more if I feel uncomfortable after eating, but I wouldn’t say I’m exercising a lot specifically to burn it off. I’m not trying to be dramatic — it just feels really urgent in the moment.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight or body shape is different from how others see it?\n\nPatient: I… yeah, I think so. I mean, I know other people probably don’t look at me and see what I see, or they’d say I’m being too hard on myself.\n\nBut when I’m in the bathroom mirror at home, it’s like all I can focus on is my stomach and my waist. If they look… wrong, or bigger than I think they should, I feel like I’ve messed up somehow, like I wasn’t disciplined enough. Even if I had a good day at the rescue center and did everything right there, that part can still make me feel like I failed.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over the past few months?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure, honestly. I mean, I check, but it kind of goes up and down, and I don’t know if I’d call it “significant.” Maybe it feels significant to me when it changes a little, but I’m not sure it would look that way to someone else.\n\nIs that… the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever exercise excessively to try to burn off calories after one of these episodes?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in a big way. I mean, I might walk around more or feel restless afterward, but I wouldn’t say I’m doing intense workouts to make up for it.\n\nAt least, I don’t think that’s what I’m doing. Is that what you mean by excessively?\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of gaining weight the main reason you try to undo the calories after eating?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that’s probably the biggest part of it, even if I don’t like admitting that.\n\nLike, if I get on the scale and it’s even a pound higher after a shift, or if my work pants feel tighter, I kind of latch onto it. I’ll tell myself it’s probably nothing, but then I spend the whole day worrying I’m letting myself get fat, and I start thinking about how I’m going to make up for it.\n\nI know that probably sounds a bit extreme. But yes, I think the fear of gaining weight is a lot of what’s behind it.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period where you didn't have these episodes of eating when you can't stop and then making yourself throw up?\n\nPatient: I guess… not really, at least not lately.\n\nFor the last few months — maybe four months or so — it’s been pretty consistent. Usually at least one night a week, when I’m by myself, I end up eating way more than I meant to, and then afterward I make myself throw up.\n\nI don’t love saying that. I keep wanting to say it’s not that bad because it’s not every day, but… yeah, it has been happening regularly.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel a lack of control during these episodes of eating when you can't stop?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I wish I could say it’s just poor planning or being hungry, but once I’m alone at night and I start eating, it kind of feels like something takes over.\n\nI’ll be telling myself, “Just put it away, stop now,” and I still keep going. It’s embarrassing to admit, but yes, it feels like I’m not really in control in those moments. Is that… is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you also restrict your food intake between these episodes of eating when you can't stop?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that.\n\nI don’t think I’m, like… formally restricting, if that makes sense? I don’t have a strict plan or anything. Sometimes I do try to “be good” the next day or eat lighter, but I haven’t really paid close attention to whether it’s a pattern.\n\nIs that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you often eat very little during the day before one of these episodes of eating when you can't stop happens?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I haven’t really tracked it that closely.\n\nI mean… some days I might be busy at work and not eat much, but I don’t know if that’s specifically before it happens or just normal being busy. I wouldn’t want to say yes if I’m not sure.\n\nI can try to pay more attention to that, if that would help.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods of significant weight loss followed by weight gain over the past year?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in any big, obvious way.\n\nMy weight maybe goes up and down a little, but I haven’t noticed, like, a major drop and then gaining it all back or anything. I don’t weigh myself super consistently, so I might not be the best judge, but nothing that stood out to me as significant.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a time when you lost a lot of weight and then gained it back?\n\nPatient: Not that I can really think of, no. I mean, my weight has probably shifted a bit here and there, but I don’t remember a time where I lost a lot and then put it all back on.\n\nI hope that answers it—I’m not trying to be vague, I just don’t think there’s been anything that dramatic.\n\nDoctor: Do you often skip meals or go for long periods without eating on purpose?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I do it on purpose, no. I mean, I might miss a meal if work gets hectic or I’m distracted, but I wouldn’t say I’m intentionally trying to go a long time without eating.\n\nAt least… not that I’ve really noticed. I try to tell myself it’s just normal concern, but if the scale is even like a pound up after a shift, or my work pants feel a little tighter, it can kind of ruin my whole day.\n\nI start thinking I’m letting myself get fat, and then I’m… planning how I’m going to make up for it. I know that probably sounds a bit much.\n\nDoctor: Is making yourself throw up mostly to prevent weight gain?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I don’t like admitting that, but after I eat like that, I usually end up in the bathroom making myself throw up because it feels like the only way to undo it before the calories… settle in, I guess.\n\nI know that probably sounds bad. I’m not trying to be dramatic — it just feels really urgent in the moment.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight or body shape is different from how others see it?\n\nPatient: I… yeah, I think so. I mean, I know other people probably don’t look at me and see what I see, or they’d say I’m being too hard on myself.\n\nBut when I’m in the bathroom mirror at home, it’s like all I can focus on is my stomach and my waist. If they look… wrong, or bigger than I think they should, I feel like I’ve messed up somehow, like I wasn’t disciplined enough. Even if I had a good day at the rescue center and did everything right there, that part can still make me feel like I failed.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over the past few months?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure, honestly. I mean, I check, but it kind of goes up and down, and I don’t know if I’d call it “significant.” Maybe it feels significant to me when it changes a little, but I’m not sure it would look that way to someone else.\n\nIs that… the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever exercise excessively to try to burn off calories after one of these episodes?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in a big way. I mean, I might walk around more or feel restless afterward, but I wouldn’t say I’m doing intense workouts to make up for it.\n\nAt least, I don’t think that’s what I’m doing. Is that what you mean by excessively?\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of gaining weight the main reason you try to undo the calories after eating?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that’s probably the biggest part of it, even if I don’t like admitting that.\n\nLike, if I get on the scale and it’s even a pound higher after a shift, or if my work pants feel tighter, I kind of latch onto it. I’ll tell myself it’s probably nothing, but then I spend the whole day worrying I’m letting myself get fat, and I start thinking about how I’m going to make up for it.\n\nI know that probably sounds a bit extreme. But yes, I think the fear of gaining weight is a lot of what’s behind it.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period where you didn't have these episodes of eating when you can't stop and then making yourself throw up?\n\nPatient: I guess… not really, at least not lately.\n\nFor the last few months — maybe four months or so — it’s been pretty consistent. Usually at least one night a week, when I’m by myself, I end up eating way more than I meant to, and then afterward I make myself throw up.\n\nI don’t love saying that. I keep wanting to say it’s not that bad because it’s not every day, but… yeah, it has been happening regularly.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel a lack of control during these episodes of eating when you can't stop?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I wish I could say it’s just poor planning or being hungry, but once I’m alone at night and I start eating, it kind of feels like something takes over.\n\nI’ll be telling myself, “Just put it away, stop now,” and I still keep going. It’s embarrassing to admit, but yes, it feels like I’m not really in control in those moments. Is that… is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you also restrict your food intake between these episodes of eating when you can't stop?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that.\n\nI don’t think I’m, like… formally restricting, if that makes sense? I don’t have a strict plan or anything. Sometimes I do try to “be good” the next day or eat lighter, but I haven’t really paid close attention to whether it’s a pattern.\n\nIs that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you often eat very little during the day before one of these episodes of eating when you can't stop happens?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I haven’t really tracked it that closely.\n\nI mean… some days I might be busy at work and not eat much, but I don’t know if that’s specifically before it happens or just normal being busy. I wouldn’t want to say yes if I’m not sure.\n\nI can try to pay more attention to that, if that would help.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods of significant weight loss followed by weight gain over the past year?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in any big, obvious way.\n\nMy weight maybe goes up and down a little, but I haven’t noticed, like, a major drop and then gaining it all back or anything. I don’t weigh myself super consistently, so I might not be the best judge, but nothing that stood out to me as significant.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a time when you lost a lot of weight and then gained it back?\n\nPatient: Not that I can really think of, no. I mean, my weight has probably shifted a bit here and there, but I don’t remember a time where I lost a lot and then put it all back on.\n\nI hope that answers it—I’m not trying to be vague, I just don’t think there’s been anything that dramatic.\n\nDoctor: Do you often skip meals or go for long periods without eating on purpose?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I do it on purpose, no. I mean, I might miss a meal if work gets hectic or I’m distracted, but I wouldn’t say I’m intentionally trying to go a long time without eating.\n\nAt least… not that I’ve really noticed. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you need to exercise a lot to burn off the calories after one of these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I’m not someone who goes and works out for hours after eating or anything like that.\n\nSometimes I might feel like I should move around more if I feel uncomfortable after eating, but I wouldn’t say I’m exercising a lot specifically to burn it off. I hope that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're afraid of losing control over your eating during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that’s probably the part that worries me the most, if I’m being honest.\n\nIt’s usually when I’m alone at night. I’ll start eating, and at first I tell myself it’s just a little bit, but then it feels like something kind of takes over. Like I’m telling myself, “Okay, put it away now,” and I just… don’t. I can’t seem to stop in that moment.\n\nI know that probably sounds bad. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Um… thanks. I guess I’m here because my eating has been feeling kind of out of hand lately. Mostly when I’m alone at night—once I start, it’s like something takes over, and I can’t really stop, even though I’m telling myself to just put the food away. I know that probably sounds… I don’t know, but it’s been worrying me.\n\nDoctor: How do you feel after one of these episodes of eating when you can't stop?\n\nPatient: I feel… really awful, honestly. Embarrassed, mostly. And panicky, like I’ve messed everything up.\n\nUm… I don’t really like saying this, but after it happens I usually go to the bathroom and make myself throw up. It feels, in the moment, like the only way to sort of… undo it before the calories settle in. I know that’s not good. I’m sorry, I’m trying to be honest.\n\nDoctor: How often do these episodes of eating when you can't stop happen?\n\nPatient: Um… I guess it’s been more than just occasional. For the last four months or so, there’s usually been at least one night a week where I’m by myself and I eat way more than I meant to. And then… afterward I make myself throw up. I know that’s not great, but that’s the pattern, I think.\n\nDoctor: Do you worry about gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, probably more than I should. I try to tell myself it’s just normal concern, but if the scale is even like a pound up after a shift, or my work pants feel a little tighter, it can kind of ruin my whole day.\n\nI start thinking I’m letting myself get fat, and then I’m… planning how I’m going to make up for it. I know that probably sounds a bit much.\n\nDoctor: Is making yourself throw up mostly to prevent weight gain?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I don’t like admitting that, but after I eat like that, I usually end up in the bathroom making myself throw up because it feels like the only way to undo it before the calories… settle in, I guess.\n\nI know that probably sounds bad. I’m not trying to be dramatic — it just feels really urgent in the moment.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight or body shape is different from how others see it?\n\nPatient: I… yeah, I think so. I mean, I know other people probably don’t look at me and see what I see, or they’d say I’m being too hard on myself.\n\nBut when I’m in the bathroom mirror at home, it’s like all I can focus on is my stomach and my waist. If they look… wrong, or bigger than I think they should, I feel like I’ve messed up somehow, like I wasn’t disciplined enough. Even if I had a good day at the rescue center and did everything right there, that part can still make me feel like I failed.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over the past few months?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure, honestly. I mean, I check, but it kind of goes up and down, and I don’t know if I’d call it “significant.” Maybe it feels significant to me when it changes a little, but I’m not sure it would look that way to someone else.\n\nIs that… the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever exercise excessively to try to burn off calories after one of these episodes?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in a big way. I mean, I might walk around more or feel restless afterward, but I wouldn’t say I’m doing intense workouts to make up for it.\n\nAt least, I don’t think that’s what I’m doing. Is that what you mean by excessively?\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of gaining weight the main reason you try to undo the calories after eating?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that’s probably the biggest part of it, even if I don’t like admitting that.\n\nLike, if I get on the scale and it’s even a pound higher after a shift, or if my work pants feel tighter, I kind of latch onto it. I’ll tell myself it’s probably nothing, but then I spend the whole day worrying I’m letting myself get fat, and I start thinking about how I’m going to make up for it.\n\nI know that probably sounds a bit extreme. But yes, I think the fear of gaining weight is a lot of what’s behind it.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period where you didn't have these episodes of eating when you can't stop and then making yourself throw up?\n\nPatient: I guess… not really, at least not lately.\n\nFor the last few months — maybe four months or so — it’s been pretty consistent. Usually at least one night a week, when I’m by myself, I end up eating way more than I meant to, and then afterward I make myself throw up.\n\nI don’t love saying that. I keep wanting to say it’s not that bad because it’s not every day, but… yeah, it has been happening regularly.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel a lack of control during these episodes of eating when you can't stop?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I wish I could say it’s just poor planning or being hungry, but once I’m alone at night and I start eating, it kind of feels like something takes over.\n\nI’ll be telling myself, “Just put it away, stop now,” and I still keep going. It’s embarrassing to admit, but yes, it feels like I’m not really in control in those moments. Is that… is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you also restrict your food intake between these episodes of eating when you can't stop?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that.\n\nI don’t think I’m, like… formally restricting, if that makes sense? I don’t have a strict plan or anything. Sometimes I do try to “be good” the next day or eat lighter, but I haven’t really paid close attention to whether it’s a pattern.\n\nIs that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you often eat very little during the day before one of these episodes of eating when you can't stop happens?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I haven’t really tracked it that closely.\n\nI mean… some days I might be busy at work and not eat much, but I don’t know if that’s specifically before it happens or just normal being busy. I wouldn’t want to say yes if I’m not sure.\n\nI can try to pay more attention to that, if that would help.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods of significant weight loss followed by weight gain over the past year?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in any big, obvious way.\n\nMy weight maybe goes up and down a little, but I haven’t noticed, like, a major drop and then gaining it all back or anything. I don’t weigh myself super consistently, so I might not be the best judge, but nothing that stood out to me as significant.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a time when you lost a lot of weight and then gained it back?\n\nPatient: Not that I can really think of, no. I mean, my weight has probably shifted a bit here and there, but I don’t remember a time where I lost a lot and then put it all back on.\n\nI hope that answers it—I’m not trying to be vague, I just don’t think there’s been anything that dramatic.\n\nDoctor: Do you often skip meals or go for long periods without eating on purpose?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I do it on purpose, no. I mean, I might miss a meal if work gets hectic or I’m distracted, but I wouldn’t say I’m intentionally trying to go a long time without eating.\n\nAt least… not that I’ve really noticed. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you need to exercise a lot to burn off the calories after one of these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I’m not someone who goes and works out for hours after eating or anything like that.\n\nSometimes I might feel like I should move around more if I feel uncomfortable after eating, but I wouldn’t say I’m exercising a lot specifically to burn it off. I hope that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're afraid of losing control over your eating during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that’s probably the part that worries me the most, if I’m being honest.\n\nIt’s usually when I’m alone at night. I’ll start eating, and at first I tell myself it’s just a little bit, but then it feels like something kind of takes over. Like I’m telling myself, “Okay, put it away now,” and I just… don’t. I can’t seem to stop in that moment.\n\nI know that probably sounds bad. But yes, I guess I am afraid of losing control when it starts.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any significant weight fluctuations over the past year, such as losing and then regaining a substantial amount of weight?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not anything I’d call substantial. My weight probably goes up and down a bit, like a few pounds here or there, but I haven’t really tracked it closely.\n\nI mean, my clothes fit a little differently sometimes, but I wouldn’t say I’ve had a big loss and then gained it all back or anything. At least not that I’m aware of. Is that helpful?\n\nDoctor: Do you often try to limit the amount of food you eat on a typical day?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that. I don’t think I’m, like, formally dieting every day or counting everything.\n\nI guess some days I’ll tell myself I should “be good” or keep things lighter, but I don’t know if that counts as trying to limit food. It’s not something I have a strict plan for, if that makes sense.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out.
